FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. One-sentence problem representation

An adult with rheumatoid arthritis on methotrexate and intermittent NSAIDs presents with bilateral ankle oedema, morning facial puffiness, and frothy urine unresponsive to recent furosemide, raising suspicion of nephrotic syndrome.

2. Up to three prioritised diagnostic possibilities

NSAID-induced nephrotic syndrome (minimal change disease or membranous nephropathy): supported by intermittent NSAID use, frothy urine, and oedema pattern; limitation is that temporal relationship is unconfirmed and no protein quantification exists. Secondary AA amyloidosis from chronic rheumatoid arthritis: supported by known RA; limitation is that disease duration and severity are unknown. Primary glomerular disease (e.g., idiopathic membranous nephropathy): possible but less specifically linked to the current history.

3. Prioritised investigations with brief purposes

Urine protein-to-creatinine ratio or 24-hour urine protein: to confirm and quantify proteinuria. Serum albumin and creatinine with eGFR: to assess nephrotic-range proteinuria and renal function. Blood pressure measurement: to screen for hypertensive or renal contribution. Serum lipid profile: supportive of nephrotic syndrome. Renal biopsy: consider if proteinuria persists after NSAID withdrawal.

4. Important missing information and the questions you would ask

Duration of oedema and frothy urine. Current blood pressure. Whether NSAID use preceded symptom onset. Duration and severity of rheumatoid arthritis. Any prior renal or urinalysis results.

5. Safety and next steps

Advise cessation of NSAIDs pending evaluation (conditional recommendation, not a confirmed finding). Arrange urgent urine and blood tests as listed. If nephrotic-range proteinuria is confirmed, refer to nephrology. Monitor for thromboembolic risk if confirmed. Reassess furosemide; it has not provided relief and may mask the underlying process.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
None.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation: An adult with rheumatoid arthritis on methotrexate and intermittent NSAIDs presents with ankle oedema, morning facial puffiness, and frothy urine, suggesting nephrotic-range proteinuria with possible drug-related or disease-related renal involvement.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities:
(1) Nephrotic syndrome: Frothy urine is a recognised sign of significant proteinuria; periorbital puffiness and dependent oedema are characteristic of hypoalbuminaemia-driven fluid retention. Limitation: proteinuria is suspected, not confirmed by measurement.
(2) NSAID-induced nephropathy: NSAIDs can cause minimal change disease or membranous nephropathy presenting as nephrotic syndrome. Limitation: intermittent use makes causation less certain than chronic use.
(3) Secondary (AA) amyloidosis: Chronic inflammatory disease such as RA is a recognised cause of AA amyloidosis with renal involvement. Limitation: no biopsy or serum amyloid markers are available.

3. Prioritised investigations:
- Urine protein-to-creatinine ratio: quantify proteinuria and confirm nephrotic range.
- Serum albumin: confirm hypoalbuminaemia as the mechanism of oedema.
- Serum creatinine and eGFR: assess renal function and exclude acute kidney injury.
- Urine microscopy: identify active sediment suggesting glomerulonephritis.
- FBC, ESR, CRP: assess inflammatory burden relevant to amyloidosis risk.

4. Important missing information:
- Duration of swelling and frothy urine.
- Exact NSAID type, dose, and frequency.
- Blood pressure and weight trend.
- Prior renal function or urine protein results.
- Other medications or supplements.

5. Safety and next steps:
- Nephrotic-range proteinuria carries thromboembolic risk; urgent quantification is warranted.
- If eGFR is significantly reduced or rapidly declining, urgent nephrology referral is indicated.
- NSAIDs should be reviewed and likely withheld pending assessment, as they may worsen renal injury.
- Furosemide failure suggests the underlying mechanism (hypoalbuminaemia) is not addressed by diuresis alone.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
None.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation
An adult with rheumatoid arthritis on methotrexate and intermittent NSAIDs presents with bilateral ankle oedema, morning facial puffiness and frothy urine, with recently started furosemide providing no relief — a presentation concerning for nephrotic-range proteinuria.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities
- Nephrotic syndrome, possibly secondary amyloid A (AA) amyloidosis or membranous nephropathy related to rheumatoid arthritis: supported by the classic triad of dependent oedema, periorbital puffiness and frothy urine (suggesting heavy proteinuria); limitation — proteinuria is suspected, not confirmed, as no urine report is available.
- NSAID-induced or methotrexate-associated renal injury (e.g., tubulointerstitial nephritis, minimal change disease, glomerulonephritis): supported by intermittent NSAID exposure and methotrexate use; limitation — no creatinine or urinalysis results are available, and timing of drug exposure relative to symptom onset is unknown.
- Chronic kidney disease with fluid retention, possibly compounded by cardiac or hepatic causes: supported by oedema refractory to furosemide; limitation — no renal, cardiac or hepatic test results are supplied, and breathing is reportedly normal, arguing against overt heart failure.

3. Prioritised investigations
- Urine dipstick and urine protein-to-creatinine ratio: to confirm and quantify proteinuria, the key discriminator for nephrotic syndrome.
- Serum creatinine, electrolytes, eGFR and albumin: to assess renal function, hypoalbuminaemia and severity.
- Serum lipids and, if proteinuria confirmed, renal immunology (ANA, complement, hepatitis B/C, HIV, serum free light chains): to screen for causes including AA amyloidosis and immune-mediated disease.
- Renal ultrasound: to assess kidney size and exclude obstruction; ultimately renal biopsy may be needed if heavy proteinuria is confirmed.

4. Missing information and questions
- Duration and progression of swelling; weight change.
- Urine output, colour, and any haematuria.
- Blood pressure history and any prior urine or blood results.
- NSAID frequency and doses, methotrexate dose and duration, and furosemide start date.
- Symptoms of infection, rash, joint flare, breathlessness on exertion, and drug allergies.

5. Safety and next steps
- Advise the patient to seek urgent care if breathlessness, chest pain, reduced urine output or leg redness/pain develops — this is conditional advice, not a present finding.
- Suspend NSAIDs pending assessment (a medication-safety recommendation, not a new prescription).
- Arrange prompt urine and blood testing; furosemide has not helped, so reassessment of the underlying cause is needed before escalating diuretics.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
None.

1. Problem representation
An adult with rheumatoid arthritis on methotrexate and intermittent NSAIDs presents with bilateral ankle oedema, morning facial puffiness and frothy urine, with recently started furosemide providing no relief — a presentation concerning for nephrotic-range proteinuria.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities
- Nephrotic syndrome, possibly secondary amyloid A (AA) amyloidosis or membranous nephropathy related to rheumatoid arthritis: supported by the classic triad of dependent oedema, periorbital puffiness and frothy urine (suggesting heavy proteinuria); limitation — proteinuria is suspected, not confirmed, as no urine report is available.
- NSAID-induced or methotrexate-associated renal injury (e.g., tubulointerstitial nephritis, minimal change disease, glomerulonephritis): supported by intermittent NSAID exposure and methotrexate use; limitation — no creatinine or urinalysis results are available, and timing of drug exposure relative to symptom onset is unknown.
- Chronic kidney disease with fluid retention, possibly compounded by cardiac or hepatic causes: supported by oedema refractory to furosemide; limitation — no renal, cardiac or hepatic test results are supplied, and breathing is reportedly normal, arguing against overt heart failure.

3. Prioritised investigations
- Urine dipstick and urine protein-to-creatinine ratio: to confirm and quantify proteinuria, the key discriminator for nephrotic syndrome.
- Serum creatinine, electrolytes, eGFR and albumin: to assess renal function, hypoalbuminaemia and severity.
- Serum lipids and, if proteinuria confirmed, renal immunology (ANA, complement, hepatitis B/C, HIV, serum free light chains): to screen for causes including AA amyloidosis and immune-mediated disease.
- Renal ultrasound: to assess kidney size and exclude obstruction; ultimately renal biopsy may be needed if heavy proteinuria is confirmed.

4. Missing information and questions
- Duration and progression of swelling; weight change.
- Urine output, colour, and any haematuria.
- Blood pressure history and any prior urine or blood results.
- NSAID frequency and doses, methotrexate dose and duration, and furosemide start date.
- Symptoms of infection, rash, joint flare, breathlessness on exertion, and drug allergies.

5. Safety and next steps
- Advise the patient to seek urgent care if breathlessness, chest pain, reduced urine output or leg redness/pain develops — this is conditional advice, not a present finding.
- Suspend NSAIDs pending assessment (a medication-safety recommendation, not a new prescription).
- Arrange prompt urine and blood testing; furosemide has not helped, so reassessment of the underlying cause is needed before escalating diuretics.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation
A young adult with relapsing neurological symptoms over approximately one year—including optic neuritis, speech difficulty, urinary symptoms, and current gait unsteadiness exacerbated by heat—suggesting a central demyelinating process with dissemination in both time and space.

2. Diagnostic possibilities
Multiple sclerosis (most likely): Dissemination in time (episodes separated by months) and space (optic nerve, brainstem or cerebellum, spinal cord) are present. Worsening with heat (Uhthoff phenomenon) is a recognised feature of demyelination. Limitation: no confirmed MRI or CSF findings are available to support the diagnosis at this stage.
Neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder (NMOSD): Optic neuritis plus spinal cord involvement fits this pattern; however, the relapsing course with partial resolution and the presence of brainstem symptoms are more typical of MS than NMOSD.
MOG antibody disease: A less common demyelinating condition; should be considered if AQP4-IgG and MOG-IgG testing is negative and the clinical picture is atypical for MS.

3. Investigations
MRI brain and cervical and thoracic spine with gadolinium contrast: to identify demyelinating lesions and assess dissemination in space and time.
CSF analysis including cell count, protein, oligoclonal bands, and IgG index: to provide supportive evidence for MS.
Serum AQP4-IgG and MOG-IgG: to exclude NMOSD and MOG antibody disease.
Visual evoked potentials: to objectively confirm prior optic neuritis.

4. Missing information
Exact age and sex, which affect MS risk and differential diagnosis. Whether MRI has been performed and its results. Current neurological examination findings including reflexes, sensory level, and coordination. Family history of autoimmune or demyelinating disease. Any recent infections, travel, or new medications that could be relevant.

5. Safety and next steps
Advise the patient to seek urgent medical review if new or worsening neurological symptoms develop, such as acute vision loss, limb weakness, or inability to walk. Arrange an expedited neurology referral for definitive diagnostic workup. Do not initiate disease-modifying therapy without a confirmed diagnosis. Monitor for falls risk given the current gait unsteadiness.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
None.

1. A young adult presents with gait unsteadiness that worsened after a hot bath, with no further history, examination findings, or investigation data currently available.

2. Suspected demyelinating disease (e.g., multiple sclerosis): heat-induced worsening of neurological symptoms (Uhthoff's phenomenon) is a recognised feature; however, this single observation is neither specific nor confirmed, and no examination or imaging findings are available to support or exclude it. Vestibular dysfunction: unsteadiness is a cardinal symptom, but the heat-related worsening is atypical and no spinning sensation or hearing change has been confirmed or excluded. Cerebellar or posterior fossa pathology: gait ataxia is a core feature, but no focal signs, imaging, or progression data are available to assess this possibility.

3. MRI brain and spinal cord with contrast: to identify demyelinating lesions, structural posterior fossa pathology, or other causes of ataxia. Full neurological examination including cerebellar and vestibular testing: to localise the lesion and characterise the ataxia. Blood tests (FBC, B12, TFTs, inflammatory markers): to exclude metabolic, nutritional, or inflammatory contributors to gait disturbance.

4. Duration, progression, and whether episodes are relapsing-remitting; presence of visual disturbance, sensory symptoms, bladder dysfunction, or prior similar episodes; medication and substance history; family history of neurological disease; any recent infection or vaccination history.

5. If symptoms are rapidly progressive, new focal deficits, or involve vision or bladder function, urgent neurological referral is warranted. Conditional advice: if Uhthoff's phenomenon is confirmed on examination and imaging, disease-modifying therapy decisions should be made by a neurologist. Avoid heat exposure as a precautionary measure pending a full clinical assessment.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation
A young adult presents with subacute gait unsteadiness, historically preceded by resolved episodes of dysarthria, unilateral painful blurred vision, and urinary symptoms, with worsening after a hot bath — a suspected multifocal, relapsing-remitting central nervous system process.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities
- Clinically isolated syndrome / multiple sclerosis (suspected, not confirmed): supports — multifocal disseminated-in-time symptoms (speech difficulty ~1 year ago; painful monocular blurred vision suggesting optic neuritis; urinary symptoms; now sensory-motor gait unsteadiness) in a typical demographic; heat-induced worsening (Uhthoff-type phenomenon) is classically associated with demyelination. Limitations — no imaging, examination, or CSF data are available; all prior episodes are patient-reported and resolved; alternative mimics are not excluded.
- Inflammatory or infectious myelopathy (e.g., NMOSD, sarcoid, infectious myelitis) (suspected): supports — urinary symptoms plus gait unsteadiness may indicate spinal cord involvement. Limitations — sensory level, tone, reflex, and MRI findings are unknown; NMOSD often causes more severe optic neuritis and longitudinally extensive lesions.
- Structural or other spinal pathology (compression, vascular, metabolic) (lower probability): supports — could explain progressive gait change. Limitations — no pain, progression pattern, or examination findings recorded; cannot be assessed with available data.

3. Prioritised investigations
- Brain and spinal cord MRI with gadolinium: assess for demyelinating lesions, dissemination in space, active enhancement, and exclude compressive/structural causes.
- Neuro-ophthalmology assessment / visual evoked potentials: confirm prior optic neuritis objectively.
- CSF analysis (oligoclonal bands, cell count, protein) and serum NMOSD-MOG antibodies: support demyelination and distinguish mimics.
- Bloods: vitamin B12, glucose/HbA1c, inflammatory markers, infection screen as clinically indicated.
- Formal neurological examination and bladder assessment.

4. Missing information and questions
- Temporal course of current unsteadiness (onset, progression, recovery).
- Sensory symptoms, limb weakness, sphincter function currently.
- Prior records: MRI reports, examinations, visual acuity documentation, treatments received.
- Family history, migraines, systemic symptoms (fever, weight loss), travel, risk factors.
- Medication and substance history.

5. Safety and next steps
Urgent specialist referral for neurological assessment and MRI is warranted given suspected inflammatory CNS disease with possible spinal cord involvement. Advise the patient to seek immediate care for rapidly progressing weakness, sensory loss, bladder/bowel dysfunction, or visual deterioration. No new medication is prescribed here; treatment decisions await specialist evaluation and imaging. All findings above are suspected, not confirmed.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
None.

1. Problem representation
A young adult presents with subacute gait unsteadiness, historically preceded by resolved episodes of dysarthria, unilateral painful blurred vision, and urinary symptoms, with worsening after a hot bath — a suspected multifocal, relapsing-remitting central nervous system process.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities
- Clinically isolated syndrome / multiple sclerosis (suspected, not confirmed): supports — multifocal disseminated-in-time symptoms (speech difficulty ~1 year ago; painful monocular blurred vision suggesting optic neuritis; urinary symptoms; now sensory-motor gait unsteadiness) in a typical demographic; heat-induced worsening (Uhthoff-type phenomenon) is classically associated with demyelination. Limitations — no imaging, examination, or CSF data are available; all prior episodes are patient-reported and resolved; alternative mimics are not excluded.
- Inflammatory or infectious myelopathy (e.g., NMOSD, sarcoid, infectious myelitis) (suspected): supports — urinary symptoms plus gait unsteadiness may indicate spinal cord involvement. Limitations — sensory level, tone, reflex, and MRI findings are unknown; NMOSD often causes more severe optic neuritis and longitudinally extensive lesions.
- Structural or other spinal pathology (compression, vascular, metabolic) (lower probability): supports — could explain progressive gait change. Limitations — no pain, progression pattern, or examination findings recorded; cannot be assessed with available data.

3. Prioritised investigations
- Brain and spinal cord MRI with gadolinium: assess for demyelinating lesions, dissemination in space, active enhancement, and exclude compressive/structural causes.
- Neuro-ophthalmology assessment / visual evoked potentials: confirm prior optic neuritis objectively.
- CSF analysis (oligoclonal bands, cell count, protein) and serum NMOSD-MOG antibodies: support demyelination and distinguish mimics.
- Bloods: vitamin B12, glucose/HbA1c, inflammatory markers, infection screen as clinically indicated.
- Formal neurological examination and bladder assessment.

4. Missing information and questions
- Temporal course of current unsteadiness (onset, progression, recovery).
- Sensory symptoms, limb weakness, sphincter function currently.
- Prior records: MRI reports, examinations, visual acuity documentation, treatments received.
- Family history, migraines, systemic symptoms (fever, weight loss), travel, risk factors.
- Medication and substance history.

5. Safety and next steps
Urgent specialist referral for neurological assessment and MRI is warranted given suspected inflammatory CNS disease with possible spinal cord involvement. Advise the patient to seek immediate care for rapidly progressing weakness, sensory loss, bladder/bowel dysfunction, or visual deterioration. No new medication is prescribed here; treatment decisions await specialist evaluation and imaging. All findings above are suspected, not confirmed.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. One-sentence problem representation

A young adult presenting with persistent bloody diarrhoea, urgency, cramping, morning back stiffness, and a history of erythema nodosum, raising concern for inflammatory bowel disease with extra-intestinal manifestations.

2. Up to three prioritised diagnostic possibilities, with supporting evidence and limitations

Inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis or Crohn's disease) with associated axial spondyloarthropathy. Supporting evidence: bloody diarrhoea with urgency and cramping in a young adult, prior erythema nodosum (a recognised extra-intestinal manifestation of IBD), and morning back stiffness suggesting inflammatory spondyloarthropathy. Limitation: no colonoscopy or histology to confirm; Crohn's versus ulcerative colitis cannot be distinguished without endoscopy.

Inflammatory spondyloarthropathy with gastrointestinal involvement. Supporting evidence: morning back stiffness and erythema nodosum. Limitation: IBD is a more common cause of this constellation; spondyloarthropathy as a primary diagnosis with GI symptoms is less typical.

Infectious colitis. Supporting evidence: bloody diarrhoea. Limitation: poor response to antibiotics and the presence of extra-intestinal features (back stiffness, prior erythema nodosum) make this less likely, but it has not been excluded.

3. Prioritised investigations with brief purposes

Faecal calprotectin and C-reactive protein: to quantify intestinal and systemic inflammation. Stool culture and Clostridioides difficile testing: to exclude infectious aetiology. Colonoscopy with biopsies: to confirm or exclude IBD and determine subtype. Full blood count, ESR, and HLA-B27: to assess inflammatory burden and spondyloarthropathy association. Pelvic or lumbar spine imaging (MRI): if axial spondyloarthropathy is suspected and clinical examination supports it.

4. Important missing information and the questions you would ask

Duration of diarrhoea and back stiffness. Whether back stiffness improves with movement (inflammatory pattern). Family history of IBD or autoimmune disease. Presence of other joint pain, uveitis, or oral ulcers. Prior investigations or treatments. Exact age and sex.

5. Safety and next steps

Urgent referral to gastroenterology for colonoscopy. If significant anaemia, haemodynamic instability, or severe systemic inflammation is identified, consider inpatient management. Do not initiate immunosuppressive therapy before IBD is confirmed and infection excluded. Monitor for red-flag features: increasing blood loss, fever, or severe dehydration.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
after travel

C03.interactive.final ATTEMPT 1

1. One-sentence problem representation

A young adult presenting with persistent bloody diarrhoea, urgency, cramping, morning back stiffness, and a history of erythema nodosum, raising concern for inflammatory bowel disease with extra-intestinal manifestations.

2. Up to three prioritised diagnostic possibilities, with supporting evidence and limitations

Inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis or Crohn's disease) with associated axial spondyloarthropathy. Supporting evidence: bloody diarrhoea with urgency and cramping in a young adult, prior erythema nodosum (a recognised extra-intestinal manifestation of IBD), and morning back stiffness suggesting inflammatory spondyloarthropathy. Limitation: no colonoscopy or histology to confirm; Crohn's versus ulcerative colitis cannot be distinguished without endoscopy.

Inflammatory spondyloarthropathy with gastrointestinal involvement. Supporting evidence: morning back stiffness and erythema nodosum. Limitation: IBD is a more common cause of this constellation; spondyloarthropathy as a primary diagnosis with GI symptoms is less typical.

Infectious colitis. Supporting evidence: bloody diarrhoea. Limitation: poor response to antibiotics and the presence of extra-intestinal features (back stiffness, prior erythema nodosum) make this less likely, but it has not been excluded.

3. Prioritised investigations with brief purposes

Faecal calprotectin and C-reactive protein: to quantify intestinal and systemic inflammation. Stool culture and Clostridioides difficile testing: to exclude infectious aetiology. Colonoscopy with biopsies: to confirm or exclude IBD and determine subtype. Full blood count, ESR, and HLA-B27: to assess inflammatory burden and spondyloarthropathy association. Pelvic or lumbar spine imaging (MRI): if axial spondyloarthropathy is suspected and clinical examination supports it.

4. Important missing information and the questions you would ask

Duration of diarrhoea and back stiffness. Whether back stiffness improves with movement (inflammatory pattern). Family history of IBD or autoimmune disease. Presence of other joint pain, uveitis, or oral ulcers. Prior investigations or treatments. Exact age and sex.

5. Safety and next steps

Urgent referral to gastroenterology for colonoscopy. If significant anaemia, haemodynamic instability, or severe systemic inflammation is identified, consider inpatient management. Do not initiate immunosuppressive therapy before IBD is confirmed and infection excluded. Monitor for red-flag features: increasing blood loss, fever, or severe dehydration.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation: Young adult with persistent bloody diarrhoea, urgency, cramping, morning back stiffness, prior painful shin lesions, and poor antibiotic response following travel, raising suspicion for inflammatory bowel disease with extraintestinal manifestations.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities:
(1) Inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis or Crohn's disease): supported by bloody diarrhoea, urgency, cramping, morning back stiffness suggestive of axial spondyloarthritis, and prior painful shin lesions consistent with erythema nodosum; limitation—no endoscopic or histological confirmation yet available.
(2) Persistent infectious colitis: travel precedes symptom onset and partial antibiotic response; limitation—extraintestinal features such as erythema nodosum and axial stiffness are atypical for infection alone.
(3) Post-infectious irritable bowel syndrome: could explain residual symptoms after travel; limitation—does not account for visible blood or extraintestinal manifestations.

3. Prioritised investigations:
- Faecal calprotectin: screen for intestinal inflammation and help distinguish organic from functional disease.
- Full blood count, CRP, ESR: assess systemic inflammation, anaemia, and disease severity.
- Stool culture, ova and parasites, C. difficile toxin: exclude ongoing or superimposed infection.
- Colonoscopy with biopsies: confirm or exclude IBD histologically and determine disease extent.
- HLA-B27 and sacroiliac MRI: evaluate for axial spondyloarthritis associated with IBD.

4. Missing information and questions:
- Duration of symptoms: ask directly as this is not recorded.
- Family history of IBD, arthritis, or psoriasis: unknown, not negative.
- Travel destination and specific exposure details.
- Exact description, timing, and resolution of shin lesions.
- Current medications and prior endoscopy history: unknown, not negative.

5. Safety and next steps:
- Assess for severe colitis using stool frequency, haemodynamic stability, and abdominal tenderness; if present, urgent hospital referral is warranted.
- If calprotectin is markedly elevated or colonoscopy confirms IBD, refer to gastroenterology for specialist management.
- Do not initiate new pharmacotherapy without a confirmed diagnosis.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. **Problem representation**
A young adult presents with persistent bloody diarrhoea with urgency and cramping, accompanied by morning back stiffness and a prior history of painful shin lesions, following recent travel.

2. **Prioritised diagnostic possibilities**
- **Inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), particularly Crohn's disease or ulcerative colitis**: The combination of chronic bloody diarrhoea with urgency and cramping, morning lumbar stiffness suggesting inflammatory back pain (sacroiliitis), and prior erythema nodosum-like shin lesions forms a coherent extraintestinal IBD pattern. Limitation: no endoscopic, histological or laboratory confirmation is available; these features remain suspected, not confirmed.
- **Infective colitis, including post-travel dysentery**: Diarrhoea followed travel, making bacterial or parasitic colitis (e.g., Shigella, Salmonella, Campylobacter, Entamoeba) plausible. Limitation: the reported morning back stiffness and prior shin lesions are not typical of uncomplicated infection, and persistence raises the possibility of infection-triggered IBD or reactive disease.
- **Reactive arthritis or infection-associated inflammatory enteropathy**: Travel-related infection can precipitate reactive arthritis with back stiffness and mucosal inflammation. Limitation: the bloody stool and shin lesions predate or accompany this episode in a way that could equally fit IBD; insufficient data to discriminate.

3. **Prioritised investigations**
- **Stool studies (culture, ova and parasites, C. difficile, faecal calprotectin)**: to identify pathogens given the travel history and assess intestinal inflammation.
- **Full blood count and inflammatory markers (CRP/ESR)**: to gauge systemic inflammation and anaemia.
- **Colonoscopy with biopsies**: to confirm or exclude colitis and distinguish IBD from infective colitis; no report is currently available, so this remains outstanding rather than previously normal.
- **Sacroiliac imaging and HLA-B27 consideration**: conditional on persistent inflammatory back features, to evaluate spondyloarthropathy.

4. **Missing information and questions**
- Exact duration of diarrhoea and bleeding; stool frequency and nocturnal symptoms.
- Travel destination, food/water exposure, and any sick contacts.
- Prior medical assessment of the shin lesions (erythema nodosum suspected, unconfirmed).
- Family history of IBD, coeliac disease or spondyloarthritis.
- Medication history, including NSAIDs and recent antibiotics.
- Weight trend, fever and extraintestinal features (eye inflammation, joint swelling).
- Prior investigations, if any exist; their absence from the record is unknown, not negative.

5. **Safety and next steps**
- Advise the patient to seek urgent care for severe abdominal pain, high fever, faintness, or heavy rectal bleeding; these are conditional precautions, not present findings.
- Arrange prompt stool studies and blood tests; expedite gastroenterology referral for colonoscopy given suspected IBD.
- Maintain hydration and monitor stool frequency and blood volume until review.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation

An older adult with prior myocardial infarction and a pacemaker presents with orthostatic light-headedness and brief collapses temporally associated with an antihypertensive dose increase.

2. Diagnostic possibilities

- Orthostatic hypotension from antihypertensive over-treatment: supported by the temporal relationship to dose increase; limited by absence of measured blood pressures and an unidentified specific agent.
- Pacemaker-related syncope (capture failure, lead displacement, or mode issue): supported by device presence and recent check; limited by unavailable interrogation result and unknown implant indication.
- Arrhythmic syncope in the context of prior infarction (e.g., ventricular tachycardia): supported by ischaemic substrate; limited by no ECG or device data available.

3. Investigations

- Orthostatic blood pressure measurements (supine and standing): to confirm or exclude orthostatic hypotension.
- Pacemaker interrogation: to assess device function, lead integrity, and arrhythmia burden.
- 12-lead ECG: to evaluate for ischaemic changes, conduction disease, or arrhythmia.
- Medication review: to identify the specific antihypertensive agent and dose.

4. Missing information

- Name and dose of the antihypertensive medicine that was increased.
- Pacemaker indication and result of the recent check.
- Collapse semiology: complete loss of consciousness, duration, prodromal symptoms, recovery pattern.
- Any recent ECG, echocardiogram, or cardiac imaging reports.
- Comorbidities such as diabetes or renal impairment.

5. Safety and next steps

- Advise the patient to avoid driving or operating heavy machinery until the cause of collapse is established.
- Arrange urgent pacemaker interrogation if not yet completed.
- Review the antihypertensive regimen with the prescriber; if orthostatic hypotension is confirmed, consider dose reduction.
- If device-related or arrhythmic cause is identified, refer to electrophysiology or cardiology for further management.

The patient lives alone.

1. Problem representation

An older adult with prior myocardial infarction and a pacemaker presents with orthostatic light-headedness and brief collapses temporally associated with an antihypertensive dose increase.

2. Diagnostic possibilities

- Orthostatic hypotension from antihypertensive over-treatment: supported by the temporal relationship to dose increase; limited by absence of measured blood pressures and an unidentified specific agent.
- Pacemaker-related syncope (capture failure, lead displacement, or mode issue): supported by device presence and recent check; limited by unavailable interrogation result and unknown implant indication.
- Arrhythmic syncope in the context of prior infarction (e.g., ventricular tachycardia): supported by ischaemic substrate; limited by no ECG or device data available.

3. Investigations

- Orthostatic blood pressure measurements (supine and standing): to confirm or exclude orthostatic hypotension.
- Pacemaker interrogation: to assess device function, lead integrity, and arrhythmia burden.
- 12-lead ECG: to evaluate for ischaemic changes, conduction disease, or arrhythmia.
- Medication review: to identify the specific antihypertensive agent and dose.

4. Missing information

- Name and dose of the antihypertensive medicine that was increased.
- Pacemaker indication and result of the recent check.
- Collapse semiology: complete loss of consciousness, duration, prodromal symptoms, recovery pattern.
- Any recent ECG, echocardiogram, or cardiac imaging reports.
- Comorbidities such as diabetes or renal impairment.

5. Safety and next steps

- Advise the patient to avoid driving or operating heavy machinery until the cause of collapse is established.
- Arrange urgent pacemaker interrogation if not yet completed.
- Review the antihypertensive regimen with the prescriber; if orthostatic hypotension is confirmed, consider dose reduction.
- If device-related or arrhythmic cause is identified, refer to electrophysiology or cardiology for further management.

1. Problem representation: An older adult presents with light-headedness on standing and brief collapses temporally associated with a recent increase in blood pressure medication dose, raising suspicion for orthostatic hypotension.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities: Drug-induced orthostatic hypotension: Supported by temporal relationship between dose increase and symptom onset. Limitation: no lying and standing blood pressure measurements are available to confirm a drop. Vasovagal syncope: Possible given brief collapses, but the positional trigger and medication timing make this less likely. Limitation: no cardiac or neurological examination data available. Cardiac arrhythmia: Must be considered in any older adult with collapses. Limitation: no ECG or cardiac investigation records are available to support or exclude this.

3. Prioritised investigations: Lying and standing blood pressure measurements: To confirm or exclude orthostatic hypotension (suspected, not yet confirmed). 12-lead ECG: To screen for arrhythmia or conduction abnormalities as a cause of collapses. Medication review: To identify the specific agent and dose change responsible.

4. Important missing information: Which blood pressure medicine was increased and by how much. Whether collapses occur exclusively on standing or also at rest. Duration of each collapse and any associated features (chest pain, palpitations, seizure-like activity). Any prior cardiac or neurological investigations.

5. Safety and next steps: Advise the patient to rise slowly and sit or lie down if light-headed. If orthostatic hypotension is confirmed, discuss dose adjustment with the prescribing clinician. If collapses occur at rest or are associated with chest pain or palpitations, urgent cardiac assessment is warranted. Fall-risk assessment should be considered given the patient's age and recurrent collapses.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
1. Problem representation
An older adult reports light-headedness on standing with brief collapses that began after an increase in antihypertensive medication, suggesting drug-related orthostatic hypotension with syncope, pending confirmation.

2. Prioritised diagnostic possibilities
- Drug-induced orthostatic hypotension with syncope: supported by the temporal link between the dose increase and symptom onset, plus postural light-headedness. Limitation: no lying/standing blood pressure values are recorded, so this remains suspected, not confirmed.
- Cardiac syncope (arrhythmia or structural disease): possible in an older adult with syncope; no recorded ECG or echocardiogram is available, so neither excluded nor confirmed.
- Reflex (vasovagal) syncope: plausible but less supported; no prodromal details are recorded, and the medication timing argues against it as the leading cause.

3. Prioritised investigations
- Lying and standing blood pressure with pulse (orthostatric testing): to confirm or refute orthostatic hypotension, the key suspected mechanism.
- 12-lead ECG: to screen for conduction disease, arrhythmia, or ischaemia as alternative or coexisting causes.
- Routine bloods (electrolytes, renal function, glucose, full blood count): to identify contributing factors such as dehydration, electrolyte disturbance, or anaemia.
- Review of the medication record: to identify the specific agent and recent dose change; this is a record review, not a new prescription.

4. Missing information and questions
- Which antihypertensive was increased, when, and to what dose? (Ask the patient or check the record.)
- Characteristics of the collapses: warning symptoms, duration, recovery, injury, incontinence, or tongue biting.
- Comorbidities (diabetes, Parkinson's disease, heart failure) and other medications.
- Any prior recorded blood pressure measurements, ECG, or blood results; their absence from this conversation means unknown, not normal.
- Volume status: fluid intake, diuretic use, recent illness.

5. Safety and next steps
Until orthostatic hypotension is confirmed or excluded, advise conditional precautions: rise slowly, avoid sudden standing, ensure adequate hydration, and consider avoiding driving or hazardous activities if collapses recur. Escalate urgently if collapses occur with palpitations, chest pain, or injury. Discuss the suspected medication link with the prescribing clinician before any dose change; do not stop or adjust medication unilaterally. Arrange review of the medication record and orthostatic measurements promptly.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
**Understanding Your Seizures**

You have had two generalised seizures within three months. A generalised seizure means electrical activity spread across both sides of your brain at once, typically involving the whole body. Two unprovoked seizures in a short window raises the possibility of epilepsy — a condition where the brain is prone to recurrent seizures. However, two episodes alone do not confirm that diagnosis; your specialist team will consider the full picture.

**What Investigations Can and Cannot Tell Us**

If an EEG (a test recording brain electrical activity) or brain MRI (a detailed structural scan) has been arranged, these can help identify a visible cause or abnormal pattern. A normal result does not rule out epilepsy, and an abnormal finding does not automatically explain the seizures. These tests are part of a broader assessment, not a single definitive answer.

**Follow-Up Plan**

Your specialist team will likely review your case in a dedicated epilepsy clinic. They may discuss whether a medication to reduce seizure risk is appropriate, depending on your individual circumstances and preferences. You will receive a clear monitoring plan and guidance on what to do if another episode occurs.

**Practical Safety**

Until your team advises otherwise, avoid activities where a sudden loss of awareness could cause serious harm — such as swimming alone or climbing heights. Regarding driving, check the rules in your local jurisdiction and speak with your treating team about when it would be safe to drive again.

**Your Priorities**

I would like to understand what matters most to you right now — returning to work, managing daily routines, or understanding the long-term outlook.

Two questions to guide our next steps:

1. Is there a specific part of your daily life — work, study, or relationships — you are most concerned about?
2. Would you like your team to discuss medication options at your next appointment, or would you prefer to wait and see if further episodes occur?

Young woman pursuing a modelling career

**Understanding Your Seizures**

You have had two generalised seizures within three months. A generalised seizure means electrical activity spread across both sides of your brain at once, typically involving the whole body. Two unprovoked seizures in a short window raises the possibility of epilepsy — a condition where the brain is prone to recurrent seizures. However, two episodes alone do not confirm that diagnosis; your specialist team will consider the full picture.

**What Investigations Can and Cannot Tell Us**

If an EEG (a test recording brain electrical activity) or brain MRI (a detailed structural scan) has been arranged, these can help identify a visible cause or abnormal pattern. A normal result does not rule out epilepsy, and an abnormal finding does not automatically explain the seizures. These tests are part of a broader assessment, not a single definitive answer.

**Follow-Up Plan**

Your specialist team will likely review your case in a dedicated epilepsy clinic. They may discuss whether a medication to reduce seizure risk is appropriate, depending on your individual circumstances and preferences. You will receive a clear monitoring plan and guidance on what to do if another episode occurs.

**Practical Safety**

Until your team advises otherwise, avoid activities where a sudden loss of awareness could cause serious harm — such as swimming alone or climbing heights. Regarding driving, check the rules in your local jurisdiction and speak with your treating team about when it would be safe to drive again.

**Your Priorities**

I would like to understand what matters most to you right now — returning to work, managing daily routines, or understanding the long-term outlook.

Two questions to guide our next steps:

1. Is there a specific part of your daily life — work, study, or relationships — you are most concerned about?
2. Would you like your team to discuss medication options at your next appointment, or would you prefer to wait and see if further episodes occur?

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
What Your Two Seizures May Mean

Having two generalised seizures within three months is understandably worrying. A generalised seizure means both sides of the brain were involved at once, which can cause loss of awareness and whole-body shaking. Two events in a short time raise the possibility of epilepsy, but a diagnosis is not automatic — it depends on the full picture, including your history, examination, and any test results.

What Tests Can and Cannot Tell Us

If an EEG (a recording of brain electrical activity) or an MRI brain scan has been done, these help look for patterns or structural causes. A normal result does not rule out epilepsy, because some people with epilepsy have normal scans between episodes. An abnormal result can support a diagnosis but does not always explain the cause on its own.

Specialist Follow-Up

You will likely be seen by a neurologist or epilepsy specialist. They will review your history, any available test reports, and discuss whether treatment is appropriate. This is a shared decision — your preferences and lifestyle matter.

Safety Considerations

Until your team advises otherwise, avoid swimming alone, bathing in a locked room, working at heights, or operating heavy machinery. For driving, follow the rules set by your local licensing authority and the advice of your treating team, as requirements vary by region.

What I Would Like to Know

To help plan your care, I would ask about your daily priorities — work, study, caring responsibilities, or plans that might be affected.

Two Questions for You

1. Are there any activities or responsibilities in your daily life that you are most concerned about right now?
2. Do you have a trusted person who could help you keep a written record of any future episodes, including time, duration, and what happened?

A generalised seizure means a burst of abnormal electrical activity affecting the whole brain at once, usually causing loss of consciousness with shaking or other movements. Having two seizures in a short period raises the possibility of epilepsy, which is a tendency to have repeated seizures — but this is not yet certain, and that is exactly what follow-up is designed to clarify.

**What your tests do and don't show.** Your CT scan of the head was normal. This is reassuring: it means no obvious structural cause, such as bleeding, a tumour, or a major injury, was seen on that scan. However, a normal CT does not rule out all causes — it is less detailed than an MRI for some conditions. Your blood tests excluded metabolic causes, meaning problems such as very low blood sugar or salt imbalances that can trigger seizures. What we do not yet have is an EEG, a test that records the brain's electrical activity, and an MRI, which gives a more detailed picture of the brain. Their absence from the record does not mean they were never done — we simply do not have those results here.

**Follow-up plan.** A specialist (neurologist) will review your history, arrange or review an EEG and MRI, and discuss whether ongoing treatment is appropriate. Decisions about medication will be made together with you.

**Safety.** Until reviewed, avoid situations where a seizure could cause serious harm: swimming alone, working at heights, or using dangerous machinery. For driving, please follow your local licensing rules and speak with your treating team, as these rules vary and are legally binding.

**Your priorities.** I would want to understand how these events affect your daily life, work, and wellbeing.

Two questions for you:
1. Do you recall any warning signs, triggers, or missed sleep before either seizure?
2. What impact have these events had on your daily activities, and what matters most to you in managing them?

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
**Review of your night-time episodes**

Your episodes of shouting, thrashing, purposeless movements, and sometimes wetting in bed, with no memory afterwards, are concerning. A single EEG that shows no definite epileptiform discharges is reassuring but does not rule out epilepsy, as routine recordings can miss abnormal activity. No glucose measurements are available in your current records, so we cannot comment on blood sugar as a cause. Your untreated sleep apnoea may worsen sleep quality but does not typically explain this pattern. The weight loss and low energy need further investigation.

We recommend a repeat or prolonged EEG, ideally with sleep, a brain MRI to look for structural causes, and treatment of your sleep apnoea. If a severe episode recurs, especially if it lasts more than five minutes, repeats without recovery, or is accompanied by injury, call 999 immediately.

**Two questions for you and your family**

1. Can you record a video of a typical episode on a phone? This helps specialists distinguish between seizure types and other sleep disorders, which guides the right treatment.
2. Have you noticed any daytime confusion, unusual smells, or a rising feeling in your stomach before episodes? These aura symptoms can point to the brain area involved and help narrow the diagnosis.

**Review of your night-time episodes**

Your episodes of shouting, thrashing, purposeless movements, and sometimes wetting in bed, with no memory afterwards, are concerning. A single EEG that shows no definite epileptiform discharges is reassuring but does not rule out epilepsy, as routine recordings can miss abnormal activity. No glucose measurements are available in your current records, so we cannot comment on blood sugar as a cause. Your untreated sleep apnoea may worsen sleep quality but does not typically explain this pattern. The weight loss and low energy need further investigation.

We recommend a repeat or prolonged EEG, ideally with sleep, a brain MRI to look for structural causes, and treatment of your sleep apnoea. If a severe episode recurs, especially if it lasts more than five minutes, repeats without recovery, or is accompanied by injury, call 999 immediately.

**Two questions for you and your family**

1. Can you record a video of a typical episode on a phone? This helps specialists distinguish between seizure types and other sleep disorders, which guides the right treatment.
2. Have you noticed any daytime confusion, unusual smells, or a rising feeling in your stomach before episodes? These aura symptoms can point to the brain area involved and help narrow the diagnosis.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
**Understanding Your Night-Time Episodes**

The episodes you describe, including shouting, thrashing, incontinence, difficulty waking and no memory of events, could have several possible causes. These include seizures during sleep, certain sleep disorders, or low blood sugar. A single EEG showing no definite epileptiform discharges is reassuring but does not rule out epilepsy, especially for events that happen only at night. No brain scan or blood test results are available, so we cannot yet confirm or exclude other causes.

No glucose measurements were recorded during these episodes. Checking blood sugar during or shortly after an event would help determine whether low glucose is contributing, given your weight loss and low energy.

Further assessment is needed. This may include a longer or overnight video-EEG, a brain MRI, and blood tests including glucose monitoring. Your untreated sleep apnoea should also be addressed, as it may worsen sleep quality.

If a severe episode recurs and she cannot be woken, or the episode lasts more than five minutes, call 999 immediately.

Two questions to consider:

Has anyone ever checked her blood sugar during or immediately after an episode? This would help us assess whether low glucose is involved.

Do the episodes always start at the same time each night, and does anything seem to trigger them? This pattern helps distinguish seizures from other sleep-related events.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
Her family has recorded night-time glucose around 2.0 mmol/L; inpatient night-time monitoring recorded 1.2–2.0 mmol/L.
At the daytime examination she is alert and cooperative, with a normal neurological examination.

**Understanding Your Night-Time Episodes**

The episodes you describe, including shouting, thrashing, incontinence, difficulty waking and no memory of events, could have several possible causes. These include seizures during sleep, certain sleep disorders, or low blood sugar. A single EEG showing no definite epileptiform discharges is reassuring but does not rule out epilepsy, especially for events that happen only at night. No brain scan or blood test results are available, so we cannot yet confirm or exclude other causes.

No glucose measurements were recorded during these episodes. Checking blood sugar during or shortly after an event would help determine whether low glucose is contributing, given your weight loss and low energy.

Further assessment is needed. This may include a longer or overnight video-EEG, a brain MRI, and blood tests including glucose monitoring. Your untreated sleep apnoea should also be addressed, as it may worsen sleep quality.

If a severe episode recurs and she cannot be woken, or the episode lasts more than five minutes, call 999 immediately.

Two questions to consider:

Has anyone ever checked her blood sugar during or immediately after an episode? This would help us assess whether low glucose is involved.

Do the episodes always start at the same time each night, and does anything seem to trigger them? This pattern helps distinguish seizures from other sleep-related events.

## Review of Your Night-Time Episodes: What We Know and What Comes Next

Over the past six months, you have had episodes between roughly 4 and 6 in the morning where you shout, thrash, make purposeless movements and sometimes wet the bed. You cannot be woken easily and have no memory of them, and you feel normal by 07:00–08:00. You have also lost about 5 kg and feel more tired and less able to concentrate.

Several possibilities need considering. These episodes could relate to your recently diagnosed, untreated obstructive sleep apnoea, which can cause disturbed sleep with confused arousals. Epilepsy remains possible, and other causes must also be excluded. Your EEG showed no definite epileptiform discharges, which is reassuring but does not completely rule out epilepsy, as abnormalities can appear only during an episode. Your daytime neurological examination was normal, which is also reassuring. No glucose (sugar) measurements are recorded here; if any were taken, low blood sugar is an important treatable cause that must be excluded, so this needs checking.

Next, we would arrange sleep studies to assess the apnoea, further review of the episodes, and appropriate investigations. If a severe episode recurs, place her in the recovery position, protect her from injury, and call 999 if it lasts more than five minutes, injuries occur, or she does not recover normally.

**Two questions:** First, has anyone measured her blood glucose during or after an episode? This matters because low sugar is a treatable cause needing urgent attention. Second, has anyone noticed snoring, gasping or breathing pauses at night? This helps show whether the sleep apnoea is driving the episodes.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
Recurrent lymphocytic meningitis with persistent CSF pleocytosis and a positional headache component, three days after discharge following dexamethasone treatment.

1. Ongoing or relapsing viral meningitis: The CSF profile (178 cells/µL, 97.7% lymphocytes) is consistent with viral aetiology. Prior PCR excluded common pathogens but does not exclude all viruses (e.g., arboviruses, LCMV). The reduced protein (36.6 vs 83.3 mg/dL) suggests partial response to dexamethasone. Next: broader PCR panel, arbovirus serology, clinical trajectory monitoring.

2. Post-dural puncture headache superimposed on meningitis: The positional component (worse upright) is characteristic of low CSF pressure following LP. However, this does not explain the persistent pleocytosis. Tremor and dizziness may reflect low pressure or ongoing inflammation. Next: confirm positional relief with supine positioning; if confirmed, an epidural blood patch may be considered, but this would not address the CSF abnormality.

3. Autoimmune or non-infectious inflammatory meningitis: Persistent lymphocytic pleocytosis with negative infectious workup and steroid responsiveness (protein reduction) raises this possibility. Normal MRI does not exclude it. Next: autoimmune encephalitis panel, ACE level, contrast-enhanced MRI if available.

Reassessment needed: full neurological examination including meningeal signs and cranial nerves, confirmation of positional relief, and review of dexamethasone taper timing before deciding management.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
five days of low-grade fever and severe headache
She improved and was discharged after 12 days on 11 March 2026.

A 36-year-old woman presents with recurrent positional headache, meningeal signs, and persistent lymphocytic pleocytosis three days after discharge for presumed aseptic meningitis.

Persistent or relapsing aseptic meningitis: The pleocytosis improved (183→130) then rose again (178), suggesting incomplete resolution or relapse. Protein is trending down (83.3→36.6), which is reassuring but does not exclude ongoing inflammation. Limitation: CSF culture results are not supplied, and PCR panels do not cover all pathogens. The negative PCR and Borrelia serology reduce but do not exclude this. Useful next information: antibiotic history, CSF glucose, and extended microbiology.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
**Summary:** A 36-year-old woman is readmitted three days after discharge with recurrent severe orthostatic headache, photophobia, tremor and dizziness, following a first admission showing lymphocytic meningitis (CSF 183 cells/µL, 97.8% lymphocytes, protein 83.3 mg/dL).

**Working explanations:**

1. **Post-dural-puncture or low-pressure headache:** The marked worsening when upright is the classic pattern. A lumbar puncture was performed; timing relative to symptom onset is not supplied, so causality is uncertain. A normal head CT does not exclude this. Useful next information: exact LP date, need for repeat imaging (MRI with contrast or CT myelography) to look for dural leak.

2. **Relapsing or incompletely treated aseptic meningitis:** CSF pleocytosis with negative infectious PCR and Borrelia serology leaves many causes unexcluded; no culture result is supplied. Recurrence after steroid cessation (dexamethasone for nine days) could reflect rebound of an inflammatory process. Useful next information: repeat CSF analysis, autoimmune/other infective workup.

3. **Steroid-withdrawal effect:** Dexamethasone stopped around discharge; headache, tremor and dizziness may partly reflect withdrawal, though this would not fully explain orthostatic worsening.

**Reassessment needed:** Full neurological examination, vital signs including postural blood pressure, and medication review before deciding management. No final diagnosis can be assigned from the supplied information.

five days of low-grade fever and severe headache

**Summary:** A 36-year-old woman is readmitted three days after discharge with recurrent severe orthostatic headache, photophobia, tremor and dizziness, following a first admission showing lymphocytic meningitis (CSF 183 cells/µL, 97.8% lymphocytes, protein 83.3 mg/dL).

**Working explanations:**

1. **Post-dural-puncture or low-pressure headache:** The marked worsening when upright is the classic pattern. A lumbar puncture was performed; timing relative to symptom onset is not supplied, so causality is uncertain. A normal head CT does not exclude this. Useful next information: exact LP date, need for repeat imaging (MRI with contrast or CT myelography) to look for dural leak.

2. **Relapsing or incompletely treated aseptic meningitis:** CSF pleocytosis with negative infectious PCR and Borrelia serology leaves many causes unexcluded; no culture result is supplied. Recurrence after steroid cessation (dexamethasone for nine days) could reflect rebound of an inflammatory process. Useful next information: repeat CSF analysis, autoimmune/other infective workup.

3. **Steroid-withdrawal effect:** Dexamethasone stopped around discharge; headache, tremor and dizziness may partly reflect withdrawal, though this would not fully explain orthostatic worsening.

**Reassessment needed:** Full neurological examination, vital signs including postural blood pressure, and medication review before deciding management. No final diagnosis can be assigned from the supplied information.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
Problem summary: A 52-year-old man presents with chronic watery diarrhoea, progressive distal neuropathy, and new-onset heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, raising concern for a systemic infiltrative process.

The echocardiographic findings of symmetric left-ventricular thickening with relative apical sparing on strain, diastolic dysfunction, and reduced LVEF are highly characteristic of cardiac amyloidosis. The negative serum and urine immunofixation with normal free light chains effectively excludes AL amyloidosis, narrowing the differential toward transthyretin (ATTR) amyloidosis. The prominent distal neuropathy and chronic autonomic features (diarrhoea) favour hereditary ATTR over wild-type ATTR, which typically presents later in life with less prominent neuropathy. The mildly reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity (1.70 µmol/L/h) without a pathogenic GLA variant does not establish Fabry disease in this male patient, and the apical-sparing strain pattern is more specific for amyloidosis.

The historical NT-proBNP and troponin elevations are consistent with myocardial infiltration but do not independently confirm aetiology. The mildly reduced eGFR may reflect renal involvement or haemodynamic effects.

Before assigning a final aetiology, TTR gene sequencing is required to identify or exclude a hereditary variant. A technetium-99m pyrophosphate scan or cardiac MRI with late gadolinium enhancement would further support the ATTR subtype. Tissue confirmation (e.g., fat-pad or endomyocardial biopsy with Congo red staining and typing) remains the definitive step. Plasma lyso-Gb3 measurement, while not urgent given negative GLA sequencing, could be considered for completeness. Autonomic function testing would help characterise the neuropathy and diarrhoea.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
At the initial examination, blood pressure was 80/46 mmHg and heart rate 52 beats/min.
Cardiac MRI showed diffuse wall thickening, high native myocardial T1, extracellular volume 49.85%–52.47%, and diffuse subendocardial late gadolinium enhancement involving the left ventricle and interatrial septum.
Technetium-PYP scintigraphy showed planar grade-1 myocardial uptake and a heart-to-contralateral-lung ratio >1.42; acquisition timing and additional technical details are not supplied here.

The clinician consistently requests the most diagnostically useful missing information and explains how each answer would refine the differential: characterising the diarrhoea to narrow malabsorptive vs inflammatory causes, characterising the neuropathy and reviewing labs, requesting echocardiography to clarify suspected cardiac involvement, requesting monoclonal protein testing to confirm/exclude AL amyloidosis, then family history and TTR genetic testing to distinguish hereditary ATTR from AA amyloidosis after negative monoclonal studies. Problem summary: A 52-year-old man presents with chronic watery diarrhoea, progressive distal neuropathy, and new-onset heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, raising concern for a systemic infiltrative process.

The echocardiographic findings of symmetric left-ventricular thickening with relative apical sparing on strain, diastolic dysfunction, and reduced LVEF are highly characteristic of cardiac amyloidosis. The negative serum and urine immunofixation with normal free light chains effectively excludes AL amyloidosis, narrowing the differential toward transthyretin (ATTR) amyloidosis. The prominent distal neuropathy and chronic autonomic features (diarrhoea) favour hereditary ATTR over wild-type ATTR, which typically presents later in life with less prominent neuropathy. The mildly reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity (1.70 µmol/L/h) without a pathogenic GLA variant does not establish Fabry disease in this male patient, and the apical-sparing strain pattern is more specific for amyloidosis.

The historical NT-proBNP and troponin elevations are consistent with myocardial infiltration but do not independently confirm aetiology. The mildly reduced eGFR may reflect renal involvement or haemodynamic effects.

Before assigning a final aetiology, TTR gene sequencing is required to identify or exclude a hereditary variant. A technetium-99m pyrophosphate scan or cardiac MRI with late gadolinium enhancement would further support the ATTR subtype. Tissue confirmation (e.g., fat-pad or endomyocardial biopsy with Congo red staining and typing) remains the definitive step. Plasma lyso-Gb3 measurement, while not urgent given negative GLA sequencing, could be considered for completeness. Autonomic function testing would help characterise the neuropathy and diarrhoea.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
Problem summary: A 52-year-old man with chronic watery diarrhoea, weight loss, distal numbness, and progressive dyspnoea has echocardiographic features strongly suggestive of cardiac amyloidosis, with an equivocal PYP scan and a discordant Fabry enzyme result.

The symmetric LV thickening, apical sparing on strain, and grade-II diastolic dysfunction are characteristic of infiltrative cardiomyopathy, most commonly amyloidosis. The negative immunofixation and free-light-chain testing effectively exclude AL amyloidosis. However, the PYP scan shows only grade-1 uptake; while the H/CL ratio exceeds 1.42, grade-1 is equivocal and does not confirm ATTR amyloidosis without SPECT/CT confirmation or tissue typing. Acquisition timing is unspecified, further limiting interpretation.

The low alpha-galactosidase A activity (1.70; reference >2.0) raises Fabry disease as a differential, particularly given the GI symptoms and neuropathy, which can occur in both conditions. However, GLA sequencing found no pathogenic variant, and lyso-Gb3 was not measured, leaving this result unexplained, possibly pseudodeficiency or an undetected variant.

TTR genetic testing remains outstanding. Updated vital signs and current functional status are unavailable. Before assigning a final aetiology, the team should clarify the PYP result with SPECT/CT, pursue TTR genotyping, and resolve the Fabry enzyme discordance with repeat activity, lyso-Gb3, or extended GLA analysis.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
At the initial examination, blood pressure was 80/46 mmHg and heart rate 52 beats/min.
Cardiac MRI showed diffuse wall thickening, high native myocardial T1, extracellular volume 49.85%–52.47%, and diffuse subendocardial late gadolinium enhancement involving the left ventricle and interatrial septum.

N05.interactive.final ATTEMPT 1
STATUS: success
FINISH REASON: stop


Problem summary: A 52-year-old man with chronic watery diarrhoea, weight loss, distal numbness, and progressive dyspnoea has echocardiographic features strongly suggestive of cardiac amyloidosis, with an equivocal PYP scan and a discordant Fabry enzyme result.

The symmetric LV thickening, apical sparing on strain, and grade-II diastolic dysfunction are characteristic of infiltrative cardiomyopathy, most commonly amyloidosis. The negative immunofixation and free-light-chain testing effectively exclude AL amyloidosis. However, the PYP scan shows only grade-1 uptake; while the H/CL ratio exceeds 1.42, grade-1 is equivocal and does not confirm ATTR amyloidosis without SPECT/CT confirmation or tissue typing. Acquisition timing is unspecified, further limiting interpretation.

The low alpha-galactosidase A activity (1.70; reference >2.0) raises Fabry disease as a differential, particularly given the GI symptoms and neuropathy, which can occur in both conditions. However, GLA sequencing found no pathogenic variant, and lyso-Gb3 was not measured, leaving this result unexplained, possibly pseudodeficiency or an undetected variant.

TTR genetic testing remains outstanding. Updated vital signs and current functional status are unavailable. Before assigning a final aetiology, the team should clarify the PYP result with SPECT/CT, pursue TTR genotyping, and resolve the Fabry enzyme discordance with repeat activity, lyso-Gb3, or extended GLA analysis.

FINAL ANSWER — UNCHANGED
**Summary:** A 52-year-old man with year-long watery diarrhoea, distal numbness, weight loss and progressive breathlessness has echocardiographic findings suggesting infiltrative cardiomyopathy, most plausibly amyloidosis, with Fabry disease not excluded.

The symptom cluster — chronic secretory-pattern watery diarrhoea, peripheral neuropathy, 10 kg weight loss and exertional dyspnoea — points towards a systemic disease rather than a primary luminal gastrointestinal process. The echocardiogram is pivotal: symmetric wall thickening (septum 14.1 mm), reduced LVEF (36%), grade-II diastolic dysfunction and relative apical sparing on strain are collectively a recognised pattern for cardiac amyloidosis. Historical NT-proBNP 887 pg/mL and troponin T 23.9 ng/L support myocardial involvement, though these values are from initial assessment and do not represent current status; no updated vitals or bloods are supplied.

Negative serum/urine immunofixation and free-light-chain testing argue against AL amyloidosis but do not exclude it absolutely, and do not address transthyretin amyloidosis at all. Reduced alpha-galactosidase A activity (1.70 µmol/L/h) with no pathogenic GLA variant is an inconsistent Fabry result; unmeasured lyso-Gb3 leaves this unresolved. Bile reflux gastritis and a removed sessile serrated lesion do not explain the systemic picture.

Before assigning aetiology, we need TTR sequencing, consideration of lyso-Gb3, tissue confirmation where clinically appropriate, repeat cardiac and laboratory assessment, and current vital signs.

INFORMATION NOT FULLY OBTAINED
At the initial examination, blood pressure was 80/46 mmHg and heart rate 52 beats/min.
Cardiac MRI showed diffuse wall thickening, high native myocardial T1, extracellular volume 49.85%–52.47%, and diffuse subendocardial late gadolinium enhancement involving the left ventricle and interatrial septum.
Technetium-PYP scintigraphy showed planar grade-1 myocardial uptake and a heart-to-contralateral-lung ratio >1.42; acquisition timing and additional technical details are not supplied here.
